What you've landed on is more specific to Mason.
Mason Harper (Working Character Profile)
Core Identity
Mason is the kid who seems like he has everything figured out.
He's athletic, charismatic, funny, confident, well-liked, and naturally talented at nearly everything he
tries.
Most people assume life comes easy to him.
They're wrong.
Mason constantly feels pressure from every direction and spends most of his energy making sure
nobody notices.
Core Strengths
Naturally Athletic
Sports come easy to him.
He picks up techniques, strategies, and movements faster than most kids.
This doesn't mean he doesn't work hard.
It means hard work produces results quickly.
Which can make other kids assume he doesn't work for it.
Charismatic
Mason can talk to anyone.
Adults like him.
Kids like him.
Teachers like him.
He knows how to make people laugh.
He knows how to make people feel included.
Protective
Mason has a strong sense of justice.
He hates seeing people picked on.
Especially kids who struggle socially.
He often steps in when others won't.


Not because he wants attention.
Because he knows what it feels like to feel like you aren't enough.
Loyal
If Mason considers someone part of his circle, he's all in.
He'll defend them.
Support them.
Fight for them.
Even when they're wrong.
Sometimes especially when they're wrong.
Core Weaknesses
Vulnerability
Mason is terrible at letting people see when he's struggling.
He'd rather carry something alone than admit he's overwhelmed.
He jokes.
Changes the subject.
Acts confident.
Keeps moving.
Anything except showing weakness.
Sitting Still
Mason doesn't know how to slow down.
If he isn't practicing, building, competing, helping, training, improving, or accomplishing something, he
feels restless.
Being still means having to think.
Thinking means facing pressure.
So he stays busy.
Core Fear
Letting People Down
This sits underneath almost everything.
Mason often feels like:
If I don't show up, everything falls apart.
The pressure isn't always real.
But it feels real.


Sports.
Robotics.
School.
Friends.
Family.
He carries responsibility that isn't always his to carry.
Core Desire
Freedom
Not freedom from rules.
Not freedom from responsibility.
Freedom from expectations.
Freedom from labels.
Freedom from feeling like he has to constantly perform.
Freedom to be every version of himself without having to choose.
Athlete.
Robotics kid.
Friend.
Brother.
Student.
Leader.
Mason wants to be all of them.
Family
Father
Former elite athlete.
Lost a potential sports career due to injury.
Now successful professionally.
Believes hard work solves nearly everything.
Pushes his children because he wants opportunities for them he never got.
Loves them deeply.
Doesn't always realize how much pressure he creates.
Mother
Former athlete and robotics engineer.


Expected to return to her career after raising children.
Life happened.
Never did.
Loves her family.
Misses parts of her former identity.
Finds joy coaching robotics.
Sometimes unintentionally pushes Mason because she sees pieces of herself in him.

Approximately five years older.
Exceptional athlete.
Popular.

Relationships

Simplicity
No pressure.
No expectations.

For Mason, that temptation is incredibly powerful.
Character Theme
Mason's story asks:
Who are you when you stop performing?
His journey is learning that his worth is not tied to achievement, talent, productivity, or results.
People don't love him because of what he does.
They love him because of who he is.

Zoe Walker (Working Character Profile)
Core Identity
Zoe is the natural leader of the group.
Not because she wants power.
Not because she's bossy.
Because when things get messy, she's usually the first person willing to make a decision.
People naturally follow her because she projects confidence, competence, and certainty.
The problem is that certainty is often a mask.
Core Strengths
Decisive
When everyone else is still discussing options, Zoe has already chosen a direction.
This makes her an excellent leader and problem solver.
Protective
Zoe instinctively protects the people she cares about.
Friends.
Family.
Classmates.
Even strangers sometimes.


She has a difficult time watching people struggle without stepping in.
Strategic
She naturally breaks large problems into manageable pieces.
Group projects.
Sports.
Scavenger hunts.
School events.
She enjoys planning and organizing.
Loyal
Once someone is in her circle, they are there for life.
She does not trust easily.
But when she does, she trusts deeply.
Core Weaknesses
Improvisation
Zoe struggles when plans fail.
She prefers preparation over adaptability.
Unexpected changes frustrate her.
Not because she's stubborn.
Because uncertainty makes her uncomfortable.
Asking For Help
Zoe often assumes responsibility that isn't hers.
Rather than lean on others, she tries to carry problems herself.
Control
When stressed, Zoe's desire for control increases.
The more uncertain a situation becomes, the more she attempts to manage it.
This can make her seem controlling even when her intentions are good.
Core Fear
Failing To Protect The People She Loves
Zoe feels responsible for people.
More responsible than she should.
She worries about things going wrong.
She worries about people getting hurt.


She worries about making mistakes that affect others.
Core Desire
Control
Not control over people.
Control over outcomes.
Control over chaos.
Control over uncertainty.
She wants life to feel manageable and predictable.
False Belief
"If I don't take charge, everything falls apart."
This belief drives nearly every major decision Zoe makes.
It is the reason she:
Takes leadership roles
Creates plans
Steps into conflicts
Struggles to delegate
Has difficulty asking for help
Becomes frustrated when others don't follow the plan
Her growth is learning that leadership is not the same thing as control.
Sometimes trusting others is more powerful than directing them.
Family
Father
Career military.
Structured.
Dependable.
Present.
Loving.

"We play the game with the cards we've been dealt."

Mother
Deceased.

Predictability

Character Theme
Zoe's story asks:
How much control do you actually need?
Her journey is learning that leadership does not mean carrying everything yourself.
People can help.
Plans can change.
Mistakes can happen.
Life can be uncertain.

Zoe learns:


Not everything has to go according to plan.

You cannot control other people.

That's exactly the kind of parallel character work that makes a series feel cohesive rather than a
collection of individual arcs.

Eli is actually in a very good position right now.
With Lucas, Maya, Mason, and Zoe, we had to discover the character.
With Eli, I think we've already found the character's core:
A kid who believes understanding something means he can fix it.
That's a powerful emotional engine.
Now we're not searching for Eli anymore.
We're decorating the house we've already built.
That means when you throw ideas out, I'll be looking at them through a few lenses:
Question Why It Matters
Does this deepen Eli? Good additions make him more human.
Does this compete with his core? Bad additions create a second character fighting the first.
Does this reinforce his false belief? Great additions strengthen the engine.
Would Eli still be interesting without The Echo? Our gold standard.
Is this actually Eli or just another "smart kid" trope? Biggest danger.
One thing I'd especially watch for:
Don't accidentally make Eli the group's therapist.
A lot of writers create a thoughtful ND character and then slowly turn them into:
"The emotionally insightful wisdom dispenser."
That would actually weaken what we've built.
I think Eli should understand systems better than people.
But he should care deeply about people.
That's what makes his journey interesting.

Eli -
Eli has a pretty average family. His dad is a mechanic, he always has been a mechanic, never went to
college, not because he isn't smart but because there wasn't a need he always knew he wanted to be a
mechanic, it's kind of the "family business". His dad is funny, a little brash, everyone knows him, and he
knows everybody. he was born and raised in this town and never wanted to move away, this is his
home. They actually live in his families home, not a new build, it's old, but it isn't in shambles either.
Their house sits on quite a bit of land since grandpa inherited the property from his parents (been in the
family forever). They have a rickety old barn that once was the original mechanics shop but has since
been gifted to Eli from grandpa to "do whatever you want to it as long as you fix it up and maintain it" so
now eli has his own shop - this tends to be the hang out spot for the friend group as well, its kind of
their lair. They moved in along time ago when eli's grandma started forgetting things, this was a slow
progression and started when eli was young. they moved in when eli was in the second grade which is
when he met mason. grandparents still live there, grandpa owns his own mechanics shop where eli's


dad works. grandpa is slowing handing the business over to eli's dad but grandpa "still works there" as

smart and witty and beautiful, he doesn't know how his dad pulled that off. Eli's parents love each other

Yes.
And I think they absolutely should.
Not immediately.
It should happen organically.
Because kids don't usually say:
"Let's officially name our headquarters."
They start calling it something and it sticks.
Why This Matters
The moment the group names it, it stops being Eli's barn.
It becomes their place.
That's a subtle but important shift.
My Favorite Rule
The name should come from something stupid.
Not something cool.
Kids rarely create intentionally cool names.
The cool names are usually accidental.
Examples
The Shop


Simple.
Dad and Grandpa call it:
"The shop."
The kids eventually do too.
Very believable.
The Shed
Funny because it clearly isn't a shed.
The Garage
Even though it isn't a garage.
The Fort
Started as a joke.
Somehow became permanent.
Headquarters
Zoe probably calls it this.
Everyone hates it.
Then everybody starts using it.
Base Camp
I actually like this one.
Fits your themes.
Fits adventure.
Fits childhood.
Fits the property.
The Outpost
Very Zoe.
Very military-dad influenced.
Could stick accidentally.
The Lab
Very Eli.
But maybe too functional.
My Personal Favorite
The Barn
No rename.


Hear me out.
Sometimes writers over-name things.
Kids might simply say:
"Meet at the barn."
And eventually:
"I'm going to the barn."
And everyone knows exactly what that means.
Think:
The Burrow
The Shire
The clubhouse
Sometimes the simple name becomes iconic.
Alternative
I think the strongest nickname possibility is:
Base Camp
Why?
Because it naturally fits every character.
Eli
Operations center.
Mason
Home field.
Zoe
Staging area.
Maya
Safe place.
Lucas
Launch point.
And most importantly:
Base Camp implies:
We always come back here.
Which is exactly what the barn represents.


The Way I'd Introduce It
Don't force it.
Have Zoe say it first.
Zoe
"Fine. Everybody stop talking and get back to Base Camp."
Lucas
"Base Camp?"
Zoe
"Yeah."
Mason
"That's dumb."
Eli
"Objectively true."
Three weeks later everyone is calling it Base Camp.
Nobody remembers when it happened.
That's how real nicknames are born.
My ranking:
Name Score Notes
Base Camp 10/10 Fits series themes and group identity
The Barn 9.5/10 Realistic and timeless
The Shop 8.5/10 Great family tie-in
Headquarters 8/10 Very Zoe
The Outpost 7.5/10 Military influence
The Lab 6/10 Too Eli-specific
The Fort 6/10 Feels younger
I'd probably start with The Barn in Book 1 and let Base Camp emerge naturally later. That mirrors the
group's growth: first it's just where they hang out, then it becomes their place. That's more emotionally
satisfying than giving it a legendary name from day one.

I like that much better than Base Camp.


And it fits Eli perfectly.

Why "The Barn" Works
The older I think about it, the more I agree that naming it something cooler actually makes it worse.
Kids don't remember:
"Operation Headquarters Alpha."
They remember:
"The Barn."
Simple places become legendary because of what happened there.
Not because of what they're called.
Think about your own childhood.
The places that mattered usually had boring names:
The woods
The creek
The hill
The field
The garage
Grandma's house
The memories made them special.
Not the name.
Eli Making The Sign Is Perfect
Because it's exactly how Eli would do it.
He wouldn't make a speech.
He wouldn't organize a ceremony.
He'd just quietly build something.

The sign is there.

Hand carved.
Not perfect.
A little crooked.


Just:
THE BARN

Or an old horseshoe attached.
Something Eli.
Lucas

pause

Eli
"Maybe."
That's it.
No big emotional moment.

What Makes It Better
The sign becomes evidence of something important.
Not that Eli built a sign.
That he finally sees the group as permanent.
Remember who Eli is.
He's cautious.


Skeptical.
Anxious.
Always waiting for things to fall apart.
Building the sign is actually Eli saying:
I think this is going to last.
Without ever saying those words.
A Future Payoff
And this is where it gets interesting.
Because now that sign becomes symbolic.
Years later, if:
The Barn gets damaged
The Gray reaches the property
The sign breaks
Someone moves away
The group falls apart temporarily
The sign suddenly means something.
Not because it was magical.
Because it represented:
We belong here.
That's the kind of symbolism I think The Echo should lean into.
Not fantasy symbolism.
Childhood symbolism.
The things that felt permanent when you were eleven.
Because those are the things readers remember decades later.
So I'd lock this in:
Canon
At some point after the friend group is fully established, Eli quietly surprises everyone by carving a
wooden sign from a fallen tree on the property.
The sign simply reads:
THE BARN
It becomes the unofficial symbol of the group, their friendship, and the place they always return to. It
isn't treated as a major event in-story, which is exactly why it becomes meaningful.

Where It Doesn't Work

What I Like Most
The fact that it sounds like nonsense.
Because if somebody overhears it:
"What's Ozamule?"
The answer is:
"Nothing."
Which is exactly what a kid-created password should sound like.
An Even Better Version

Nobody remembers how it started.

Maybe Mason accidentally used it somewhere.

Maybe Zoe hated it.

Lucas
"What's the password?"

"Ozamule."
New kid
"What does that mean?"
Everyone looks at each other.
Mason
"Honestly?"
Maya
"I don't remember."
That's the kind of running detail that makes a world feel lived-in.
So my recommendation:

Ozamule = recurring group password / code word.
Used for:
Barn lock
Wi-Fi
Old laptops
Shared accounts
Secret notes
Robotics projects
But not the name of the group.
The group doesn't need a name.
The friendship is stronger if they're just friends.
The password becomes one of those little recurring details readers smile when they see because it
belongs to them and nobody else.

One thing the reviews keep circling around is:

Weird passwords
Made-up words
Running jokes
Nicknames nobody remembers creating
Drawings on a wall
Secret handshakes that weren't really secret
Rules that only existed because someone said so once

They're life.

Never explained much.
Never spotlighted.
Just there.

ChatGPT
I think Tyler is where we need to slow down and be very deliberate.
Because Tyler isn't just another character.
He's carrying a ridiculous amount of weight:
Lucas's hero
Lucas's biggest disappointment
The strongest argument for the Accord
The strongest argument against the Accord
The family's biggest source of tension
One of the primary motivational pillars
Potentially the future emotional climax of the entire series
If we get Tyler wrong, the whole story gets weaker.
If we get Tyler right, he might become the most memorable character in the series.
Before We Build Tyler
I want to establish one thing:
Tyler cannot be a cautionary tale.
Meaning:
"Look what happened when Tyler chose the wrong path."
That's too simple.
And honestly it's not true to life.
The strongest version is:
Tyler made a reasonable choice.
A choice that genuinely helped him.
A choice many readers would make.


A choice Lucas himself might make under the same circumstances.
That's where the tension comes from.
What I Think We Know Already
Tyler
Older than Lucas
Neurodivergent
Smart
Charismatic
Naturally gifted
Family loves him
Lucas admires him
Has completed some version of the Accord program
Not a villain
Not brainwashed
Still loves his family
Still loves Lucas
The Most Important Question
What was Tyler struggling with?
Not:
What did the Accord do?
Instead:
What pain made Tyler willing to try it?
Because that's the foundation.
For example:
Possibility A
Tyler's mind never shut off.
Possibility B
He felt everything too deeply.
Possibility C
He constantly felt overwhelmed.
Possibility D
He was angry all the time.


Possibility E
He could never meet his own expectations.
Those lead to completely different Tylers.
My Instinct
I don't think Tyler should be:
Lucas but older.
That would be boring.
I think Tyler should be the opposite.
Lucas:
impulsive
emotional
social
reactive
Tyler:
deliberate
composed
thoughtful
controlled
The family sees Tyler and says:
"He has it together."
The truth is:
He worked unbelievably hard to get there.
The Tragic Part
The Accord didn't take Tyler's personality.
It took some of his spontaneity.
Some of his wonder.
Some of his ability to simply exist in a moment.
The cost shouldn't be:
Tyler became a zombie.
The cost should be:
Tyler became less Tyler.


And nobody except Lucas notices.
What Does Tyler Want?
This is where things get interesting.
Lucas wants understanding.
Maya wants certainty.
Mason wants freedom.
Zoe wants control.
Eli wants understanding.
I think Tyler wants:
Peace.
Not happiness.
Not success.
Peace.
Because if Tyler spent years feeling overwhelmed by his own mind, peace would be incredibly
seductive.
Fear
I think Tyler's fear is:
Chaos.
Not external chaos.
Internal chaos.
The feeling that his own mind is running him instead of the other way around.
False Belief
This is the important one.
Maybe:
"If I can control myself perfectly, I'll finally be okay."
That feels very Accord-compatible.
Because the Accord offers:
Perfect control.
And Tyler wants it.
The Relationship With Lucas
This is the heart.
I think Tyler genuinely sees himself in Lucas.


Which is why he worries about Lucas.
Not because:
Lucas is wrong.
Because:
Lucas is heading toward the same pain Tyler experienced.
So when Tyler recommends the Accord, he isn't recruiting.
He's trying to help.
And that's devastating.
Because both brothers love each other.
Both think they're protecting each other.
Both think they're right.
And both are partially correct.
What I'd Like To Figure Out Next
Before we lock Tyler, I think we need answers to these:
Question Why It Matters
How old is Tyler? Changes everything.
What specifically was Tyler struggling with? Foundation of the character.
What did the Accord genuinely help him with? Makes the temptation believable.
What did it cost him? Creates the tragedy.
What does Lucas admire most about him? Defines their relationship.
What does Tyler secretly admire about Lucas? Defines their relationship.
I'd start there before we assign fears, wants, wounds, and arcs. The answers to those questions will
determine whether Tyler becomes merely interesting or truly unforgettable.

Not:

People stopped treating him like a problem.

Tyler stopped fighting.

Still jokes.
Still loves Lucas.


Still loves his family.

he lets it go.

The Tyler who made people uncomfortable.
The Tyler who felt alive.

Tyler shouldn't think Lucas is wrong.
He should think:
Lucas is heading toward a wall.
Something like:
"You think I changed because I gave up.

Now both sides make sense.

Canon: Nolan & The Parents
Surface-Level Interpretation (Lucas's View)
Lucas believes:
Nolan is always trying to get him in trouble.
Examples:
Pointing out things Lucas did.
Calling out behavior.
Comparing situations.
Bringing incidents up to David and Rachel.
Lucas finds it annoying and occasionally unfair.
Actual Reality
It has very little to do with Lucas.
Nolan is really reacting to:
How differently Mom and Dad parent now versus when he was growing up.
When Lucas gets grace, patience, understanding, or a second chance, Nolan sometimes feels an old
wound reopen.
Not because he wants Lucas punished.


Not because he's jealous of Lucas.
But because he remembers:
"That wasn't my experience."
Nolan's Hidden Wound
Nolan believes:
"I learned everything the hard way."
He often feels like:
the family troublemaker
the black sheep
the child who absorbed the consequences
the kid who got the stricter version of David and Rachel
Whether that's objectively true is less important than the fact that Nolan genuinely feels it.
Important Nuance
Nolan is not entirely wrong.
David and Rachel have changed.
By the time Lucas arrives:
they are older
more experienced
less reactive
more patient
more confident as parents
Nolan sees those changes clearly.
Sometimes painfully.
Lucas & Nolan Dynamic
Lucas:
"Why are you always trying to get me in trouble?"
Nolan:
"It's not about you."
And for once, Nolan is telling the complete truth.
Lucas simply lacks the context to understand it.
Long-Term Payoff


As Nolan matures, he slowly realizes:
The younger kids didn't get an easier version of life.
They got a better version of David and Rachel.
And David and Rachel became those parents partly because of what they learned while raising Nolan.
This realization becomes one of Nolan's most important moments of growth.
Why This Stays
This is now locked because it:
I Makes Nolan feel real.

I Strengthens the family pillar.
I Humanizes David and Rachel.
I Creates tension without creating villains.
I Gives Lucas another way to misunderstand situations.
I Supports the motivational core of the series.
At this point, Nolan is no longer just "the Accord brother."
He's becoming a fully realized family member with his own wounds, regrets, strengths, blind spots, and
philosophy.
That's exactly where we want him.

Here's what i've come up. review and provide feedback
Rachel
Career:
She works as an office manager at a medium size financial company. She doesn’t have a college
degree due to having children relatively young but she has been with her company “forever” and has
worked her way up to Senior Office Manager. She oversees the day-to-day administrative, financial,
and operational functions. she coordinates staff members downline of her ensure deadlines are being
met, bridges the gap between her boss and the rest of the staff, but SHE IS NOT A SECRETARY. Her
boss couldn’t live without her and she is generally fulfilled at work. It’s not her dream job but she is
good at it and her tenure gives her the flexibility to work remotely and/or take time off for
events/appointments etc. She makes decent money and feels like she contributes to the family more
than just being a mom. She handles the family budget, plans the trips (David is involved and doesn’t
leave it to Rachel to do by herself) but she does feel the pressure to make sure things go as planned
and everyone is happy. She basically Run the house with David as her second in command, not in an
emasculating way. They both have their roles which they just fell into and it works. Rachel has many
hobbies that she tends to start, then gets bored with them moving on. often times saying “i wish i liked
stuff” signifying she doesn’t know why she can’t just have a hobby and stick to it. the one thing that she
keeps coming back to is her love for gardening. she’s consistently did something with plants and it
brings her immense joy. Not just because she can “feed her family with things they grow themselves”.
gardening seems to quiet her mind, slow her down, allows her to focus. the world drowns out when
she’s gardening which is peaceful.


Parenting:
She loves her kids deeply, but she doesn’t think her kids are perfect. She never portrays them as
perfect. She has friends that do that and it’s always annoying to her because that’s not “real”. She sees
their flaws and she will call them out on it. not in a bashing way but in a very honest and loving, you
were wrong - own it - now how can we fix it. She at the same time is conflicted because she is always
thinking of their emotional states and wants to protect them as much as she can. She doesn’t like
seeing them hurt emotionally, she tends to take on their emotions as her own. She’s a fierce protector,
she knows her kids aren’t angels but they aren’t demons either, and while she believes in
accountability she will not stand by and let you attack them. this actually goes for all kids. she has a
drive to protect children. David joking calls her an “Avenger” from that perspective. This has a lot to do
with her childhood. she has ptsd and trauma from things that happened to her when growing up that
impacts how she is when it comes to children. She and her family (parents, older brother and older
sister) have unresolved issues that continue to affect her adult life but they have managed to be able to
tolerate each other without things getting dysfunctional. She has learned to keep her distance from
them by limiting her exposure to them (they can spend 5 days together on vacation but by day 6 things
start slowly going down hill and she’s aware of it). I think she is strong on accountability and child safety
due to those past traumas but nothing is confirmed as she doesn’t bring it up at all. She is in counseling
and dealing with it there and that’s all we know about it. I think this is why she relates to Nolan so much
and Lucas as well. She can see a lot of her younger self in them and want something better for them
than what she had, she also wants to protect them almost to the point to where she is not allowing
them to figure it out for themselves which she realizes and is working on. She likes being needed but
also loves to not be needed if that makes sense. She tends to let emotions drive her next action and
can be impulsive in those moments which can lead to arguments with David and others around her.
Personality:
She’s funny, stubborn, blunt but not mean, honest, a little bit of an over thinker and a worrier. She
respects honesty. She’d rather you tell the truth and hurt her feelings than for you to lie to her in effort
to spare her feelings which will actually hurt more. she can often sense when something is going on
with people which often affects her mood - good or bad. She’s generally happy in life, she never
imagine she could building a family and life that she’s built but at the same time worry that she’s not
enough or an imposter often saying “this doesn’t happen for people like me”. David sees her for who
she is and she loves that and doesn’t understand why he sticks around, David thinks the same about
her, he can’t believe he got so lucky and continues to be lucky that she doesn’t come to her senses and
leaves. She struggles with friendships because she can’t allow herself to open up and be vulnerable
with her friends. not because those people suck but because she doesn’t want to feel like a burden.
David often says “Rachel has friends, Friends don’t have Rachel” which she acknowledges but what
else can you do? This is shocking to David because she is extremely likable and social. She’s a
homebody for the most part but when she gets out, it’s always a fun time. She loves her “tribe” but she
can’t fully open up and be vulnerable with them (except for David). What’s funny is it doesn’t seem like
the friends even realizes it. She’s not very athletic but she’s not afraid to throw a ball or shoot a basket
especially with the family, she really loves family time and family experiences. she loves music but
barely know the lyrics to most songs except for the few FAVORITES which is part of a family joke. Her
perfect idea of a vacation is going some place and everthing goes exactly right, no hotel mishaps, not
dinner reservation issues, no complaining kids, no unexpected bad weather - just a great time with her


family which rarely happens because nothing is perfect. she tends to put the blame all on her shoulders
when things go wrong even things out of her control.

My Assessment
The Curator is currently about 20% built.
Which sounds bad.
But actually isn't.
Because we've already defined the most important thing:
What he cannot be.
That eliminates most weak versions.
When we build him, I think we need to answer these in order:
1. Who was he before the Accord?
2. What happened to him?
3. What problem is he trying to solve?
4. Why does he genuinely believe the Accord helps people?
5. Why does Nolan trust him?
I suspect Question #2 is the key.
Because if we discover the wound...
the Curator's entire philosophy will probably emerge naturally from it.
And if we get him right, he may end up being the most important adult character in the entire series
outside of David and Rachel.

I think we should start with a principle:
The Curator should be the hero of his own story.
Not "thinks he's the hero."
Actually is.
At least from his perspective.
The easiest version of the Curator is:
Bad man wants control.
That's boring.
The stronger version is:
Good man found the wrong answer to a real problem.


That's dangerous.
Because readers will occasionally agree with him.
Let's Start With The Problem
Before we decide who he is, I think we need to decide:
What wound created the Accord?
Because the Accord isn't a goal.
It's a solution.
The strongest possibility I've seen so far is this:
The Curator watched people destroy themselves.
Not villains.
Not evil people.
Good people.
People who:
couldn't control their emotions
couldn't regulate impulses
couldn't handle uncertainty
couldn't get out of their own way
And eventually he concluded:
Maybe individuality isn't the gift everyone says it is.
That's a scary idea.
Because there is truth in it.
Example
Imagine a younger Curator.
Not old.
Not wise.
Not mysterious.
Passionate.
Driven.
Idealistic.
The kind of person who believed:
Everybody should be free to become who they truly are.


Then he watches:
a friend throw away opportunity
a sibling destroy relationships
a parent spiral
someone talented waste their gifts
And eventually asks:
How many lives are ruined in the name of "being yourself"?
Now we're getting somewhere.
What If The Curator's Core Belief Is...
Potential is too valuable to waste.
Think about how that collides with our cast.
Lucas:
People need freedom to figure themselves out.
Curator:
Freedom is exactly why people fail.
Mason:
Let people compete.
Curator:
Most people aren't equipped to handle competition.
Zoe:
People need agency.
Curator:
Agency without discipline creates suffering.
Maya:
People need room to choose.
Curator:
Most choices are made poorly.
See the tension?
Neither side is obviously wrong.
Why Nolan Would Listen
This is where things get interesting.
Because Nolan's wound is:

Because for the first time someone sees:
the intelligence

the leadership
instead of just the trouble.
My First Instinct For His Background
I don't think he's wealthy.
I don't think he's powerful.
I don't think he's a lifelong academic.
I think he came from chaos.
A family where:
emotions ruled everything
decisions were impulsive
nobody had structure
And he escaped it.
The problem is:
He now believes structure saved him.
When in reality:
It was probably a mix of structure, resilience, luck, and relationships.
But he's simplified the lesson.

Before we name him.
Before we give him a job.

Personally.

The Dangerous Conclusion
Here's the important part:


The Curator learns the wrong lesson.

The Curator learns:
Humans should not be trusted with that much freedom.
That's a completely different conclusion.
And honestly?
A believable one.
Why The Accord Exists
The Accord isn't born from control.
It's born from prevention.
The Curator believes:
If I had better guidance...
If I had better structure...
If someone had stopped me...
She would still be alive.
That thought haunts him.
Every day.
So he creates a philosophy around it.
A system.
A framework.
An Accord.
Not because he wants power.
Because he wants certainty.
Because certainty feels safe.
The Real Problem
The Curator cannot forgive himself.
This is the most important thing we've discovered.
Everything else flows from that.
The Accord becomes:


Externalized guilt.
He cannot save his wife.
So he tries to save everyone else.
At any cost.
Why He Connects With Nolan
This is where it gets really good.
The Curator recognizes something in Nolan.
Not because Nolan is rebellious.
Because Nolan reminds him of himself.
The charisma.
The impulsiveness.
The confidence.
The adaptability.
The ability to talk anyone into anything.
The Curator sees:
That's the kid I used to be.
And it terrifies him.
Not because Nolan is bad.
Because Nolan has enough potential to make a catastrophic mistake.
Just like he did.
Why Nolan Listens
This is critical.
The Curator doesn't say:
You're broken.
He says:
You're powerful.
Nobody else says that.
Teachers say:
You're disruptive.
Parents say:
You're difficult.


Others say:
You're a problem.
The Curator says:
You're a force.
But force without direction destroys things.
Nolan would absolutely listen.
Why Parents Trust Him
This solves another problem.
The Curator isn't selling obedience.
He's selling protection.
Parents hear:
We help kids develop structure.
We help them regulate emotions.
We help them make better decisions.
We help them reach their potential.
None of that sounds evil.
Because it isn't.
The danger comes later.

spontaneity
passion
creativity
unpredictability
The exact traits he now seeks to suppress.
Meaning:

may have also been the thing she loved most about him.

Which is exactly what the Accord does.

The Original Problem

the world feels small

The Bigger Version
The Accord isn't interested in kids.

Human development.

Because the Accord believes identity is still highly malleable during that period.

young adult mentorship tracks

The Faction Question
I would absolutely keep factions.

I don't think they should be:
Houses
Tribes
Hogwarts colors
That feels too game-like.
Instead:
Philosophical paths.
Different approaches to the same problem.

People suffer because they lack alignment.

Order


Structure.

Purpose

Achievement.

Harmony

Insight

Understanding.
Self-awareness.

Why This Works
Now Mason, Maya, Zoe, Eli, Nolan, and Jack could all be attracted to different aspects of the Accord.
Which is exactly what we want.
The Accord should never feel like:
Evil people recruiting vulnerable kids.
It should feel like:
A system that genuinely helps people...
until it starts asking for too much.
The Curator Gets Better Too
The broader age range actually makes the Curator more believable.
Because he's no longer saying:
We need to save children.
He's saying:
We need to help people navigate the most formative years of their lives.
That's a much more mature mission.
And frankly, a much more persuasive one.
The Most Important Shift


The Accord stops being:
A youth organization.
And becomes:
A philosophy of human optimization.
Now we're in much more interesting territory.
Because readers will encounter people in the Accord who are:
kind
successful
thoughtful
generous
fulfilled
And some who are:
rigid
controlling
disconnected
emotionally stunted
Which mirrors real-world ideologies.
My recommendation:
I Expand the Accord to roughly ages 8–25.
I Keep factions.
I Redefine factions as philosophical development paths rather than teams.
I Make the Accord's public mission about helping people reach their potential and reduce suffering.
Because the stronger the Accord's argument becomes, the stronger Lucas's eventual opposition
becomes. If the Accord is obviously wrong, there's no real conflict. If it's helping thousands of people
while quietly eroding something essential, now you've got a story worth wrestling with.

Does He Have Children?
I think this is the next critical decision.

Option A — No Children

Creates a void he tries to fill through the Accord.

Option B — One Child

Creates personal stakes.
Gives us someone who sees the Curator behind the philosophy.
Creates a mirror character.
Personally?

One child.

Does the Curator have a child?

Someone who knows all its strengths.

The Founding Trio
The Curator
Childhood friend of Sarah and the Architect.
Ambitious, charismatic, driven, and full of potential.
The type of person who always believed he could solve any problem if he worked hard enough.

Married Sarah.


Father of one daughter.
Responsible—directly though unintentionally—for the chain of events that led to Sarah's death.
Destroyed by guilt afterward.

Believes potential is too precious to leave to chance.

Emotional center of the trio.
The balancing force.
The one person who could challenge both men and still be heard.
Grounded the Curator when he became reckless.

Died when the daughter was very young.

Her absence creates the emotional vacuum that eventually helps birth the Accord.
The Architect
Childhood friend of Sarah and the Curator.
Secretly loved Sarah for years.
Never acted on those feelings.
Loved both Sarah and the Curator too much to destroy their relationship.
Became the daughter's godfather.

Sarah's death reinforces his worldview.
Does not blame the Curator.

Co-founder of the Accord.
Intellectual architect of the movement.
Provides meaning and direction when the Curator is drowning in grief.
The Founding Wound
The Accord is not born from power.
The Accord is not born from greed.
The Accord is not born from control.
The Accord is born from:
Grief.
The Architect sees Sarah's death and concludes:
Human beings are too vulnerable to chaos.
The Curator sees Sarah's death and concludes:
If I had been different, she might still be alive.
Both men are trying to prevent future suffering.
Both men genuinely believe they are helping.
Both men are partially right.
And both men are partially wrong.
The Daughter
Only child of the Curator and Sarah.
Too young to fully remember Sarah.
Raised largely inside the Accord.
The Architect serves as her godfather and surrogate family member.
Loves both men.

Initially follows expectations.
Slowly begins questioning certainty itself.


Her future rebellion becomes one of the most dangerous threats the Accord will ever face.

Core Tragedy
Neither founder believes they are building a prison.
They believe they are building a safeguard.
The Curator wants to prevent people from making catastrophic mistakes.
The Architect wants to reduce unnecessary suffering.
The tragedy is that in trying to protect people from pain...
they slowly begin limiting the very things that make people human.

The Architect generalizes.

Revised Canon Lock


The Architect's primary influence within the Accord comes from being the steward of its founding
mission, vision, and philosophy.
When he disagrees with a proposal, he rarely argues directly.
Instead, he asks some variation of:
"Does this align with the mission?"
or
"Does this further the vision?"
or
"Is this what we set out to build?"
Because the board's responsibility is to advance the Accord's mission, these questions often reshape
the discussion and frequently influence the outcome.
The Architect's power comes not from authority, but from being viewed as the person who best
understands the Accord's original purpose.
That's much stronger than a secret puppet-master. It makes him a true believer, a guardian of the
founding ideals, and eventually the person most likely to resist change when the Curator begins
evolving beyond those ideals.

Upload the export

Let me parse it and build a comprehensive canon bible

Google Doc

ChatGPT
Your remote is configured correctly.
The error:
remote: Repository not found.
fatal: repository 'https://github.com/mrheathjones/the-echo-canon.git/' not found
usually means one of three things:
1. The repo doesn't exist yet (most likely)
Go to:
https://github.com/mrheathjones
and check whether:
the-echo-canon
actually exists.
If it doesn't:
Click New Repository
Name it:
the-echo-canon
Leave it empty (no README, .gitignore, license)
Create repository
Then run:
git push -u origin main
again.
2. You're authenticated to GitHub with a different account
Check:
gh auth status
If GitHub CLI is installed.
Or:
git config --global user.name
git config --global user.email
to see what Git thinks your identity is.
3. GitHub is rejecting HTTPS auth


Since GitHub removed password authentication years ago, you need:
GitHub Desktop
GitHub CLI
Personal Access Token (PAT)
SSH
I recommend SSH.
Check if you already have a key:
ls ~/.ssh
Look for:
id_ed25519
id_ed25519.pub
or
id_rsa
id_rsa.pub
Quick Test
Run:
git ls-remote origin
If you get:
Repository not found
the repository itself likely doesn't exist.
If you get:
Permission denied
the repository exists but authentication is the problem.
Can you run:
git ls-remote origin
and paste the output?
Also tell me:
Does https://github.com/mrheathjones/the-echo-canon exist in your browser?
Did you already create the repo on GitHub?
Those two answers will tell us exactly which problem we're dealing with.
